The experiments suggest that ordinary-space $k$-means routing is a strong
practical primitive, especially when points are assigned to several nearby
centers. The theoretical lesson is more specific: the useful object is not a
partition, but an overlapping certified filter cover with controlled query
stabbing number.

A certified ball filter is a triple $(P_i,o_i,R_i)$ with
\[
   P_i\subseteq P\cap \Ball(o_i,R_i).
\]
Its query region is the inflated ball
\[
   Q_i=\{q:\|q-o_i\|\le R_i+r\}.
\]
If $p\in P_i$ and $\|p-q\|\le r$, then $q\in Q_i$ by the triangle inequality.
Thus any cover in which every data point belongs to at least one stored filter
is automatically routing-correct if the query probes all filters whose inflated
balls contain $q$.

For a flat terminal cover the relevant load is
\[
   \operatorname{Stab}_{\rm scan}(q)
   =
   \sum_{i:q\in Q_i}|P_i|.
\]
For a recursive score-and-ball cover the theory suggests the power-stabbing
load
\[
   \operatorname{Stab}_F(q)
   =
   \sum_{i:q\in Q_i}|P_i|^{\rho}\Psi(R_i),
   \qquad
   \operatorname{Store}_F
   =
   \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i).
\]

\begin{lemma}[Certified cover compiler]\label{lem:certified-cover-compiler}
   Suppose a family of certified ball filters covers $P$ and satisfies, for
   every query $q$,
   \[
      \operatorname{Stab}_F(q)\le L,
      \qquad
      \operatorname{Store}_F\le S_0.
   \]
   If every active filter is searched by a terminal or recursive subroutine with
   query cost $O(|P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\polylog n)$ and storage
   $O(|P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)\polylog n)$, then the resulting node is
   routing-correct for every near pair and contributes query work
   $O(L\polylog n)$ and storage $O(S_0\polylog n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\|p-q\|\le r$ and choose any filter $i$ containing $p$. Since
   $P_i\subseteq\Ball(o_i,R_i)$,
   \[
      \|q-o_i\|\le\|q-p\|+\|p-o_i\|\le r+R_i,
   \]
   so the query activates filter $i$. Therefore one active subproblem contains
   the true near point. The work and storage bounds are exactly the displayed
   stabbing and storage sums, multiplied by the polylogarithmic overhead of the
   subroutines. Final candidates are verified in the original metric.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Certified cover with residual scan]\label{lem:cover-residual-scan}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:certified-cover-compiler}, suppose the stored
   filters cover only $P\setminus U_0$, where $U_0\subseteq P$ is a residual
   block that every query scans directly.  If
   \[
      \sum_{i\in I(q)} |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\le L
      \qquad\text{for every query }q,
   \]
   and $|U_0|\le L_0$, then the node is routing-correct and contributes query
   work
   \[
      O((L+L_0)\polylog n)
   \]
   and storage
   \[
      O((S_0+|U_0|)\polylog n),
   \]
   where $S_0=\sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\|p-q\|\le r$.  If $p\in U_0$, the always-scanned residual block
   verifies it directly.  Otherwise $p$ lies in some stored filter, and the
   proof of \cref{lem:certified-cover-compiler} shows that the query activates
   a child containing $p$.  The displayed work and storage bounds add the
   residual scan and residual storage to the certified-cover accounting.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Guard-restricted cover compiler]\label{lem:guard-restricted-cover}
   Work in one node with resident set $P$, $|P|=m$, and define the resident
   guard density
   \[
      g(q)=\frac1m\bigl|\{x\in P:\|x-q\|\le cr\}\bigr|.
   \]
   Fix a threshold $\gamma>0$.  Suppose a residual cover as in
   \cref{lem:cover-residual-scan} is routing-correct for every near pair, but
   its active-power certificate is required only on the low-guard domain
   \[
      \Omega_{\rm low}=\{q:g(q)<\gamma\}.
   \]
   Thus, for every $q\in\Omega_{\rm low}$,
   \[
      \sum_{i\in I(q)} |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\le L,
      \qquad |U_0|\le L_0 .
   \]
   There is a capped query procedure with work
   \[
      O((\gamma^{-1}+L+L_0)\polylog n)
   \]
   and the same storage as the residual cover, such that every near pair is
   found with constant probability.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   First sample
   \[
      T=\left\lceil \gamma^{-1}\log 4\right\rceil
   \]
   resident points uniformly and verify them in the original metric.  If any
   sampled point is within distance $cr$ of the query, return it.  If not, run
   the cover decoder with a cap: enumerate active filters, but abort this query
   copy before recursing if the accumulated active power would exceed $L$.
   Always scan the residual block $U_0$ before recursing into the enumerated
   filters.

   If $q\notin\Omega_{\rm low}$, then $g(q)\ge\gamma$, so the guard misses all
   valid points with probability at most
   \[
      (1-\gamma)^T\le e^{-\gamma T}\le\frac14 .
   \]
   Hence this high-guard query succeeds with probability at least $3/4$ before
   any cover work is needed; if the guard misses, aborting is harmless for
   constant-success ANN.

   If $q\in\Omega_{\rm low}$ and $\|p-q\|\le r$, then the low-guard
   active-power certificate implies that the capped decoder does not abort and
   lists every active filter.  If $p\in U_0$, the residual scan finds it.
   Otherwise, routing correctness puts $p$ in a listed child, and the recursive
   or terminal subroutine finds a valid answer.  The work bound is the guard
   sample size plus the capped active-power and residual costs.  Storage is
   unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Low-guard shadow compiler]\label{lem:low-guard-shadow}
   Work in one node with resident set $P$, guard density $g$, and low-guard
   domain $\Omega_{\rm low}=\{q:g(q)<\gamma\}$ as in
   \cref{lem:guard-restricted-cover}.  Define the low-guard shadow of the
   resident set
   \[
      E_\gamma
      =
      \{p\in P:\exists q\in\Omega_{\rm low}\text{ with }\|p-q\|\le r\}.
   \]
   Suppose the residual cover is required to route only low-guard near pairs:
   for every $q\in\Omega_{\rm low}$ and every $p\in P$ with $\|p-q\|\le r$,
   either $p\in U_0$ or some active stored filter contains $p$.  For
   compiler-compatible ball or Lipschitz-score filters, it is enough to cover
   the exposed set $E_\gamma\setminus U_0$, because the query slack then
   activates every filter containing such a near point.  Assume that the
   active-power certificate
   \[
      \sum_{i\in I(q)} |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\le L
   \]
   is imposed only for $q\in\Omega_{\rm low}$, and that
   $|U_0|\le L_0$.  Then the same capped query procedure as in
   \cref{lem:guard-restricted-cover} has work
   \[
      O((\gamma^{-1}+L+L_0)\polylog n)
   \]
   and finds a valid answer for every near pair with constant probability.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If $q\notin\Omega_{\rm low}$, the resident guard succeeds with probability
   at least $3/4$, exactly as in \cref{lem:guard-restricted-cover}.  On this
   event no recursive cover is used, so points outside $E_\gamma$ need not be
   stored in any child.

   If $q\in\Omega_{\rm low}$ and $\|p-q\|\le r$, then $p\in E_\gamma$ by
   definition.  The low-guard routing condition says that $p$ is either in the
   residual scan or in an active listed child.  The active-power certificate
   prevents the capped decoder from aborting on such a query, so the residual
   scan or recursive call finds a valid answer.  The work bound is unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Middle-density singleton obstruction]
   \label{lem:middle-density-singleton-obstruction}
   Fix $\rho<1/2$ and any polylogarithmic factor $L(m)$.  There are
   bounded-diameter nodes and low-guard queries for which retaining every
   exposed point as a certified singleton child has active power polynomially
   larger than $F_4(m,R)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let
   \[
      s=\left\lfloor \frac{m^{1-\rho}}{2L(m)}\right\rfloor .
   \]
   Put $s$ data points in an arbitrarily small ball around a query point $q$,
   and put the remaining points at distance greater than $cr$ from $q$ but
   still inside a parent ball of radius $R=O_c(r)$.  Then
   \[
      g(q)=\frac1m|\{x:\|x-q\|\le cr\}|
      \le \frac{m^{-\rho}}{2L(m)},
   \]
   so $q$ lies in the low-guard domain for threshold
   $\gamma=m^{-\rho}/L(m)$.

   A singleton child has certified radius $0$ and weight
   $1^\rho\Psi(0)=1$.  If every exposed singleton within distance $r$ of $q$
   is activated, the active power at $q$ is at least $s$.  Since
   $R=O_c(r)$, $F_4(m,R)=\Theta_c(m^\rho)$.  Thus
   \[
      \frac{s}{F_4(m,R)}
      \ge
      m^{1-2\rho}\log^{-O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   which is polynomially large when $\rho<1/2$.
\end{proof}

Consequently, the low-guard refinement is not by itself a sparse range-reporting
theorem.  Free preprocessing can enumerate singleton proposals and exact
residual covers, but the retained index still needs a selective activation rule,
a route-value allocation, or a posterior/rank-prefix reporter that avoids
listing every valid point in the middle-density regime
$m^\rho\ll |P\cap\Ball(q,cr)|\ll m^{1-\rho}$.

This also gives a safe way to use an empirical cover such as $k$-means without
claiming that Lloyd's objective proves anything. At a node, store an arbitrary
certified cover together with a capped decoder. On query $q$, the decoder either
lists every active filter while certifying
\[
   \sum_{i\in I(q)} |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\le L,
\]
or aborts before spending more than the chosen cap. In the first case the query
searches the listed filters and is correct by the compiler. In the second case
the query ignores the cover and runs the baseline score-and-ball node. Thus a
heuristic cover can only improve benign instances; the worst-case theorem is
still carried by the baseline unless one proves a uniform power-stabbing bound.
The open theoretical problem is therefore not to analyze Lloyd's algorithm
directly, but to construct covers that pass this certificate recursively.

\begin{assumption}[Decodable contracting power cover]\label{asm:power-cover}
   Fix $c>1$, $\rho=\rho_c$, and a parameter $\eta>0$. In every bounded-diameter
   localized node with $m$ resident points in a certified ball of radius $R$,
   either the node is terminal, or preprocessing constructs a query-decodable
   certified cover $(P_i,o_i,R_i)_{i\in I}$ with the following properties.
   \begin{itemize}
      \item Cover and radius decrease:
      \[
         \bigcup_iP_i=P,
         \qquad
         R_i\le R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}.
      \]
      \item For every query $q$, the decoder lists every filter whose inflated
      ball contains $q$,
      \[
         I(q)\supseteq\{i:\|q-o_i\|\le R_i+r\},
      \]
      in time
      \[
         d\,F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \]
      plus the listed-output cost.
      \item Unless the resident guard density at $q$ is at least
      $m^{-\rho}\log^{-O_c(1)}m$, the listed filters satisfy the power-stabbing
      contraction
      \[
         \sum_{i\in I(q)} |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)
         \le
         (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)
      \]
      for a constant $\kappa=\kappa(c)>0$.
      \item The stored cover satisfies the storage bound
      \[
         \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)
         \le
         mF_4(m,R) .
      \]
   \end{itemize}
\end{assumption}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional power-cover endpoint]\label{prop:power-cover-polylog}
   If \cref{asm:power-cover} holds uniformly for
   \[
      \eta=\Theta_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right),
   \]
   and terminal nodes are handled by resident scans or by
   \cref{thm:smooth-product-terminal}, then worst-case Euclidean ANN has
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Work after the bounded-diameter localization, so $R_0/r=\log^{O_c(1)}n$.
   At every reached node, first sample a resident guard of size
   $m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m$ and verify its points. If the guard-density
   alternative in \cref{asm:power-cover} holds, the guard succeeds with
   constant probability after increasing the logarithmic constant.

   Otherwise decode the active cover filters and recurse into all listed
   children. If $\|p-q\|\le r$ and $p\in P_i$, then
   $\|q-o_i\|\le r+R_i$, so the child containing $p$ is listed. Thus, on the
   non-guard branch, routing is deterministic; no independent success loss is
   paid at each geometric level.

   Each recursive cover edge decreases radius by at least
   $\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$, so a lineage has length
   \[
      H=O_c\!\left(\eta^{-2}\log\frac{R_0}{r}\right)
      =
      \log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   for the displayed choice of $\eta$. Let $T(m,R)$ be the expected query work
   from a node, normalized by the local potential. The decoding, guard, and
   terminal costs contribute $dF_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}n$. The active-child
   power-stabbing contraction gives
   \[
      \sum_{i\in I(q)}F_4(|P_i|,R_i)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Unrolling the recurrence over the radius levels therefore gives
   \[
      T(m,R)\le d\,\eta^{-2}F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}n
      =
      d\,F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]
   At the root, $F_4(n,R_0)=n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$.

   The storage recurrence uses the last bullet of \cref{asm:power-cover}. Since
   every child radius decreases, summing over the $H=\log^{O_c(1)}n$ radius
   levels and the polylogarithmic localization copies gives
   $dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$. Standard truncation and a constant number of
   independent guard samples give the stated capped bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Balanced fanout obstruction]\label{lem:balanced-fanout-obstruction}
   Consider one node with $m$ points and radius $R$. Suppose that, for some
   query $q$, the active certified cover contains $K\ge2$ disjoint children
   of equal size $m/K$, and each has radius at least
   $R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$ up to lower-order terms. Then its active recursive
   power satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{i=1}^K |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)
      \ge
      K^{1-\rho}\left(1-O(\eta^2)\right)F_4(m,R).
   \]
   In particular, for every fixed $\rho<1$ and sufficiently small $\eta$, such
   a balanced active fanout violates the contraction required in
   \cref{asm:power-cover}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The equal-size assumption gives
   \[
      \sum_{i=1}^K |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)
      \ge
      K\left(\frac mK\right)^\rho
      \min_i\frac{\Psi(R_i)}{\Psi(R)}\Psi(R).
   \]
   Since $R_i\ge R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$ and
   $\Psi(R)=1+(R/r)^4$, we have
   \[
      \frac{\Psi(R_i)}{\Psi(R)}
      \ge
      1-O(\eta^2)
   \]
   uniformly for $R\ge r$. The displayed lower bound follows. Because
   $K^{1-\rho}>1$ for $K\ge2$ and $\rho<1$, this is larger than
   $(1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)$ for all sufficiently small $\eta$.
\end{proof}

This lemma explains both why $k$-means works and why it is not the most
theory-native objective. Lloyd's algorithm minimizes an average squared
reconstruction error,
\[
   \sum_x \min_i\|x-o_i\|^2,
\]
whereas ANN routing cares about the inflated-ball stabbing sums above. A large
cluster with a slightly better mean can be bad because its inflated ball is hit
by many queries; a boundary point may need replication even if its squared
error to the nearest center is small.

The corresponding replacement for $k$-means is a power-stabbing cover problem:
choose centers, radii, and overlapping assignments to minimize a Lagrangian such
as
\[
   \max_{q\in\mathcal Q}
   \sum_{i:\|q-o_i\|\le R_i+r}|P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)
   +
   \lambda
   \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i),
\]
subject to covering the data, or the analogous sampled-near-pair objective when
only a bounded number of active filters is probed. Multi-assignment is then not
an ad hoc repair but the intended filtering mechanism: spend overlap on points
whose near-neighbor queries otherwise escape their closest cell.

There is a particularly clean finite surrogate for the replication part. Fix a
pool of candidate certified filters and a query rule $I(q)$, and let
$\mathcal E$ be a multiset of sampled near pairs $(x,q)$. For a set $A$ of
chosen memberships $(x,i)$, define the covered near-pair weight
\[
   \operatorname{Cov}(A)
   =
   \sum_{(x,q)\in\mathcal E} w_{x,q}\,
   \mathbf 1\{\exists i\in I(q):(x,i)\in A\}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Greedy near-pair cover]\label{lem:greedy-near-pair-cover}
   With the candidate filters and query rule fixed, $\operatorname{Cov}$ is a
   monotone submodular set function of the selected memberships. Consequently,
   if every membership has unit storage cost, the greedy rule that repeatedly
   adds the membership with largest marginal gain achieves at least a
   $(1-e^{-1})$ fraction of the best coverage achievable with the same number
   of memberships.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Monotonicity is immediate. For submodularity, compare two membership sets
   $A\subseteq B$ and one additional membership $e=(x,i)$. The marginal gain of
   adding $e$ is exactly the total weight of sampled pairs $(x,q)$ with
   $i\in I(q)$ that are not already covered. Since $B$ covers every pair covered
   by $A$, the uncovered set under $B$ is smaller, so the marginal gain under
   $B$ is at most the marginal gain under $A$.

   Let $O$ be an optimal set of $B_0$ memberships and let $A_t$ be the greedy
   set after $t$ steps. By submodularity, the total marginal contribution of the
   elements of $O$ relative to $A_t$ is at least
   $\operatorname{Cov}(O)-\operatorname{Cov}(A_t)$. Hence some element of $O$
   has marginal gain at least
   $(\operatorname{Cov}(O)-\operatorname{Cov}(A_t))/B_0$, and the greedy step
   gains at least as much. Thus
   \[
      \operatorname{Cov}(O)-\operatorname{Cov}(A_{t+1})
      \le
      \left(1-\frac1{B_0}\right)
      \bigl(\operatorname{Cov}(O)-\operatorname{Cov}(A_t)\bigr).
   \]
   Iterating for $B_0$ steps gives
   $\operatorname{Cov}(A_{B_0})\ge(1-(1-1/B_0)^{B_0})\operatorname{Cov}(O)
   \ge(1-e^{-1})\operatorname{Cov}(O)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Cost-sensitive near-pair cover]\label{lem:cost-sensitive-near-pair-cover}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:greedy-near-pair-cover}, assign every admissible
   membership $e$ a fixed positive cost $c_e$, and write
   $c(A)=\sum_{e\in A}c_e$. Let $O_B$ be the best membership set of cost at most
   $B$, after discarding memberships with $c_e>B$. Run density greedy: at step
   $t$, choose an unused membership maximizing
   \[
      \frac{\operatorname{Cov}(A_t\cup\{e\})-\operatorname{Cov}(A_t)}{c_e}
   \]
   among memberships of positive marginal gain. If the greedy procedure exhausts
   all positive marginal gains before reaching cost $B$, then it is optimal for
   budget $B$. Otherwise, if $A^+$ is the greedy set just after the first step
   with $c(A^+)\ge B$, then
   \[
      \operatorname{Cov}(A^+)\ge (1-e^{-1})\operatorname{Cov}(O_B).
   \]
   Thus allowing one extra membership gives the same approximation as the
   unit-cost greedy rule. Under the strict budget $B$, the better of the
   pre-overflow greedy set and the best single affordable membership gives at
   least
   \[
      \frac12(1-e^{-1})\operatorname{Cov}(O_B).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Put $f=\operatorname{Cov}$. At a greedy set $A_t$, submodularity and
   monotonicity give
   \[
      f(O_B)-f(A_t)
      \le
      f(A_t\cup O_B)-f(A_t)
      \le
      \sum_{e\in O_B}\bigl(f(A_t\cup\{e\})-f(A_t)\bigr).
   \]
   If the greedy procedure stops because no positive marginal gain remains,
   then every superset has the same coverage value, so the current set is
   optimal. Assume from now on that the first overflow step exists.

   Since $c(O_B)\le B$, some membership in $O_B$ has marginal density at least
   $(f(O_B)-f(A_t))/B$. The greedy membership $e_t$ has at least this density,
   so, writing $a_t=c_{e_t}$,
   \[
      f(O_B)-f(A_{t+1})
      \le
      \left(1-\frac{a_t}{B}\right)(f(O_B)-f(A_t))
      \le
      e^{-a_t/B}(f(O_B)-f(A_t)).
   \]
   Iterating until the first overflow gives
   \[
      f(A^+)\ge
      \left(1-\exp(-c(A^+)/B)\right)f(O_B)
      \ge
      (1-e^{-1})f(O_B).
   \]

   For the strict-budget statement, let $A$ be the pre-overflow set and let $e$
   be the overflowing membership. The one-element set $\{e\}$ is affordable
   because memberships of cost larger than $B$ may be discarded. By
   submodularity, the marginal contribution of $e$ after $A$ is at most
   $f(\{e\})$. Hence
   \[
      f(A^+)\le f(A)+f(\{e\}),
   \]
   so one of $A$ and $\{e\}$ achieves at least half of the one-extra-element
   value.
\end{proof}

This is a sharper diagnostic than a centroid-distance margin rule: an extra
membership only receives credit for near pairs that are still missed by the
current redundancy pattern.  For the recursive proof, however, a uniform
top-$a$ overlap is often the cleaner default.  It gives an exact storage
budget, regular memory layout, and a simple boundary-redundancy knob.  The
greedy lemma is useful when reallocating a fixed candidate pool, but the
quantity it optimizes should be read as an audit of uncovered near-pair
coverage, not as a reason to trust Lloyd's objective.

If one nevertheless pursues an additive ball cover, the theory suggests a
``power-cover Lloyd'' iteration that alternates this coverage or power-cost
assignment step with a center update reducing certified radii and inflated
stabbing.  The update is closer to a one-center or quantile-radius objective
than to the Euclidean mean.  Its assignment score for adding point $x$ to
filter $i$ is the marginal uncovered near-pair weight divided by the marginal
storage and stabbing power
\[
   \Delta_i(x)
   =
   (|P_i|+1)^{1+\rho}
   \Psi\!\left(\max\{R_i,\|x-o_i\|\}\right)
   -
   |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i),
\]
with analogous query-side power penalties when sampled queries already stab
many filters.  The radius term explains what ordinary $k$-means misses: a point
whose inclusion expands a certified ball should be peeled or replicated rather
than allowed to enlarge the query region of an entire cluster.  But this
additive-ball objective is not the preferred final target.  In concentrated
high-dimensional nodes the inflated regions $B(o_i,R_i+r)$ may blanket almost
all plausible queries, and then \cref{lem:balanced-fanout-obstruction} predicts
non-contraction no matter how the memberships are reallocated.  The selective
replacement is the score-filter formulation below.

Concretely, one implementation target is the following certified-cover
analogue of Lloyd iteration.
\begin{enumerate}
   \item Maintain a candidate family of balls $(o_i,R_i)$, initialized for
   example by $k$-means, farthest-point centers, or cap/medoid proposals.
   \item Given the current balls and a sampled near-pair set $\mathcal E$, add
   point--filter memberships by density greedy, using marginal uncovered
   near-pair weight divided by marginal power cost
   \[
      \Delta_i^{\rm store}(x)
      +
      \gamma\,\Delta_i^{\rm stab}(x),
   \]
   where $\Delta_i^{\rm store}$ is the storage increment above and
   $\Delta_i^{\rm stab}$ is the corresponding increase in sampled inflated-ball
   stabbing load.
   \item Recompute each center by minimizing a certified radius or a high
   quantile radius of its assigned points, with outliers allowed to peel into
   singleton or overlapping filters rather than forcing the whole ball to
   expand.
   \item At query time, list the inflated balls containing $q$, but execute the
   cover branch only when the listed power-stabbing sum is below the certified
   cap; otherwise fall back to the baseline score-and-ball node.
\end{enumerate}
Unlike a centroid-margin rule, this objective gives no reward for replicating a
point whose near pairs are already covered. Unlike ordinary $k$-means, it
penalizes the two quantities that enter the recursive proof: the radius
inflation caused by assignments and the number of expensive filters stabbed by
a query. The capped query rule keeps the construction safe as a heuristic
accelerator even before the uniform power-cover theorem is proved.
\Cref{prop:power-cover-polylog} is the exact theorem an additive cover would
have to instantiate. It avoids the branching score-frontier obstruction of
\cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator}; the missing content is instead a
worst-case decodable cover theorem for the inflated-ball power-stabbing
objective. \Cref{lem:balanced-fanout-obstruction} explains why this is not
ordinary balanced $k$-means: a recursive proof cannot afford a query to stab
several comparable children. It needs either very low stabbing, highly skewed
almost-total splits, randomized thinning as in
\cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction}, or a more selective activation rule than an
inflated ball.

The same point suggests a more selective replacement for $k$-means than another
centroid objective.  The weakness of a certified ball cover is that correctness
forces the query to activate the whole inflated ball $B(o_i,R_i+r)$.  In a
concentrated high-dimensional node these inflated balls can blanket nearly all
plausible queries, so the active fanout is large even when the Voronoi
partition is useful in practice.  A cap filter separates the two roles: a ball
certifies the recursive radius, while a one-sided score inequality certifies
whether a near query must activate the filter.

\begin{lemma}[Cap-slack certified cover]\label{lem:cap-slack-cover}
   Let each filter be specified by
   \[
      (P_i,o_i,R_i,u_i,\theta_i),
      \qquad \|u_i\|=1,
   \]
   with
   \[
      P_i\subseteq \Ball(o_i,R_i)
      \cap \{x:\langle u_i,x\rangle\ge \theta_i\},
      \qquad \bigcup_iP_i=P .
   \]
   On query $q$, list every filter satisfying
   \[
      \langle u_i,q\rangle\ge \theta_i-r .
   \]
   If the listed filters obey
   \[
      \sum_{i:\langle u_i,q\rangle\ge\theta_i-r}
      |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\le L,
      \qquad
      \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)\le S_0,
   \]
   then the cover compiler of \cref{lem:certified-cover-compiler} applies with
   query work $O(L\polylog n)$ and storage $O(S_0\polylog n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\|p-q\|\le r$ and choose a filter $i$ with $p\in P_i$.  Since
   $\|u_i\|=1$,
   \[
      \langle u_i,q\rangle
      \ge
      \langle u_i,p\rangle-\|p-q\|
      \ge
      \theta_i-r .
   \]
   Hence the query lists a child containing the near point.  The ball
   certificate $P_i\subseteq\Ball(o_i,R_i)$ supplies the recursive radius
   parameter, and the displayed sums are exactly the active query and storage
   potentials.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lipschitz-score filter compiler]\label{lem:lipschitz-score-cover}
   Let each filter be specified by a set $P_i$, a certified recursive ball
   $(o_i,R_i)$, a score function $\phi_i:\R^d\to\R$ with Lipschitz constant
   $L_i$, and a threshold $\theta_i$, with
   \[
      P_i\subseteq\Ball(o_i,R_i)\cap\{x:\phi_i(x)\ge\theta_i\},
      \qquad
      \bigcup_iP_i=P .
   \]
   On query $q$, list every filter satisfying
   \[
      \phi_i(q)\ge \theta_i-L_i r .
   \]
   If the listed filters obey
   \[
      \sum_{i:\phi_i(q)\ge\theta_i-L_i r}
      |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\le L,
      \qquad
      \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)\le S_0,
   \]
   then the cover compiler of \cref{lem:certified-cover-compiler} applies with
   query work $O(L\polylog n)$ and storage $O(S_0\polylog n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\|p-q\|\le r$ and choose a filter $i$ with $p\in P_i$.  Since
   $\phi_i$ is $L_i$-Lipschitz,
   \[
      \phi_i(q)\ge\phi_i(p)-L_i\|p-q\|\ge\theta_i-L_i r .
   \]
   Thus the query lists a certified child containing the near point.  The
   displayed sums are exactly the active query and storage potentials.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Shell-normalized cap cover]\label{lem:shell-normalized-cap-cover}
   Fix a center $o$ and a lower shell radius $\sigma>0$.  For
   $\|x-o\|\ge\sigma$, write
   \[
      \widehat x_o=\frac{x-o}{\|x-o\|}.
   \]
   Suppose each filter has a certified recursive ball $(P_i,o_i,R_i)$,
   a unit direction $u_i$, and a threshold $\theta_i$, with
   \[
      P_i\subseteq \Ball(o_i,R_i)
      \cap
      \{x:\|x-o\|\ge\sigma,\ \langle u_i,\widehat x_o\rangle\ge\theta_i\},
      \qquad
      \bigcup_iP_i=P .
   \]
   For a query $q$ with $\|q-o\|\ge\sigma$, list every filter satisfying
   \[
      \langle u_i,\widehat q_o\rangle
      \ge
      \theta_i-\frac{2r}{\sigma}.
   \]
   If the listed filters obey
   \[
      \sum_{i:\langle u_i,\widehat q_o\rangle\ge\theta_i-2r/\sigma}
      |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\le L,
      \qquad
      \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)\le S_0,
   \]
   then the cover compiler of \cref{lem:certified-cover-compiler} applies with
   query work $O(L\polylog n)$ and storage $O(S_0\polylog n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For points outside $\Ball(o,\sigma)$, the normalization map is
   $2/\sigma$-Lipschitz:
   \[
      \left\|
      \frac{x-o}{\|x-o\|}
      -
      \frac{q-o}{\|q-o\|}
      \right\|
      \le
      \frac{2\|x-q\|}{\sigma}.
   \]
   Hence, if $p\in P_i$ and $\|p-q\|\le r$, then
   \[
      \langle u_i,\widehat q_o\rangle
      \ge
      \langle u_i,\widehat p_o\rangle-\frac{2r}{\sigma}
      \ge
      \theta_i-\frac{2r}{\sigma}.
   \]
   Thus the query lists a certified child containing the near point, and the
   work and storage accounting is the same as in
   \cref{lem:certified-cover-compiler}.
\end{proof}

These score-cover lemmas contain both certified ball covers and cap covers.  Taking
$\phi_i(x)=-\|x-o_i\|$ and $\theta_i=-R_i$ gives the ordinary inflated-ball
rule $\|q-o_i\|\le R_i+r$, which is the worst-case-safe interpretation of a
$k$-means cell.  Taking $\phi_i(x)=\langle u_i,x\rangle$ gives
\cref{lem:cap-slack-cover}.  \Cref{lem:shell-normalized-cap-cover} gives the
formal version of the empirical observation that, after local centering and
shelling, Euclidean $k$-means behaves much like an angular quantizer: if the
norms are nearly constant, nearest-center ranking differs from ranking by
linear or normalized-cap scores only by lower-order radial terms.  Thus
$k$-means can initialize the directions, but the certificate should be stated
for overlapping cap or Lipschitz-score filters.

The theory-native alternative to $k$-means is therefore not a different
centroid objective alone.  It is a fixed-overlap score-filter tree: learn
directions or Lipschitz scores whose active sets are selective, store each
point in its top $a$ filters, and recurse only through filters passing the
slack certificate.  Redundancy amplifies a good partition, but it cannot
replace selectivity; the active-power cap is the proof-side test of whether the
learned router is useful at a node.

Thus a theory-native practical alternative is a cap-power or score-power
iteration rather than ordinary Lloyd iteration.  Its simplest filters are
spherical caps after local centering, shell normalization, or whitening, but
\cref{lem:lipschitz-score-cover} also allows learned or anisotropic Lipschitz
scores.  The default assignment is fixed top-$a$ membership by score, with the
threshold $\theta_i$ set to the worst retained score or to a certified peeled
quantile.  If additional adaptive memberships are allowed, they should be
chosen by uncovered near-pair value divided by the marginal increase in
\[
   |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)
   \quad\text{and}\quad
   \sum_{\text{sampled }q:
      \phi_i(q)\ge\theta_i-L_i r}
      |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i),
\]
while the update step should move the score parameters and the certified ball
$(o_i,R_i)$ to reduce query stabbing and child radius.  This objective can beat
plain $k$-means on instances where directional or learned scores are selective
but inflated balls are not: the query probes filters aligned with its score
direction, instead of every cluster whose enclosing ball lies within additive
distance $r$.

A concrete version is the balanced cap-power filter.  In a localized node,
first center, shell, and optionally whiten the data.  Choose directions
$u_1,\ldots,u_K$ and offsets $\theta_i$ so that the retained normalized caps
\[
   P_i=\{x\in P:\langle u_i,\widehat x_o\rangle\ge\theta_i\}
\]
have controlled masses, then attach to each retained cap its certified
recursive ball $(o_i,R_i)$.  A point may be stored in several caps; the
regular default is its top-$a$ caps, with optional greedy additions only when
they buy uncovered near-pair value per marginal storage and stabbing power.
A query lists the caps with
\[
   \langle u_i,\widehat q_o\rangle\ge\theta_i-\frac{2r}{\sigma}
\]
where $\sigma$ is the lower shell radius, or, in the more general Lipschitz case,
$\phi_i(q)\ge\theta_i-L_i r$.  The offsets play the balancing role that
$k$-means cell sizes play in practice, while the cap scores supply the
selectivity that inflated balls lose on concentrated high-dimensional data.
Flat scoring against all $K$ filters can be replaced by a recursive spherical
tree or product-code decoder; the certificate above only concerns the filters
that the decoder actually lists.  Thus ordinary $k$-means becomes an
initializer or fallback, while the proof-aligned object is a balanced
overlapping score cover whose measured quantity is active power, not squared
reconstruction error.

For training such a cover on sampled queries or sampled near pairs, the natural
loss is therefore the empirical active-power loss
\[
   \mathcal L_{\rm act}
   =
   \operatorname*{Quantile}_{q\in\mathcal Q}
   \sum_{i:\phi_i(q)\ge \theta_i-L_i r}
   |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i),
\]
together with the storage penalty
\[
   \mathcal L_{\rm store}
   =
   \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)
\]
and a penalty for sampled near pairs that are not covered by an activated
membership.  The quantile can be replaced by a maximum for a worst-query
certificate, or by a high quantile for a practical router with a capped
fallback.  A Lloyd-like implementation would alternate between balancing the
offsets, assigning fixed top-$a$ memberships by score, adding any extra
memberships by the greedy near-pair-cover rule of
\cref{lem:cost-sensitive-near-pair-cover}, and updating the score parameters to
decrease $\mathcal L_{\rm act}+\lambda\mathcal L_{\rm store}$ while recomputing
the certified radii.  Plain $k$-means is the special case that uses the wrong
surrogate loss, namely average squared reconstruction error; the score-power
variant trains directly against the quantity that controls recursive query
work.

The easiest part of this program to make self-contained is the seeding step.
Instead of running Lloyd updates, use a $k$-means++-style residual sampler to
propose cap directions, and then certify every proposed cap by scanning the
node.

\begin{lemma}[Residual cap seeding]\label{lem:residual-cap-seeding}
   Work in one shell around $o$ and write
   $\widehat x_o=(x-o)/\|x-o\|$.  Let $a_x\ge0$ be arbitrary residual weights
   on the current resident set, let $a(P)=\sum_{x\in P}a_x>0$, and sample
   $T$ independent candidate directions by drawing $X_t\in P$ with probability
   $a_x/a(P)$ and setting $u_t=\widehat X_{t,o}$.  Suppose a set
   $A\subseteq P$ satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in A}a_x\ge \alpha a(P)
   \]
   and has shell-normalized diameter at most $\eta$,
   \[
      \|\widehat x_o-\widehat y_o\|\le\eta
      \qquad\text{for all }x,y\in A .
   \]
   Then, with probability at least $1-e^{-\alpha T}$, one sampled direction
   $u_t$ satisfies
   \[
      A\subseteq
      \left\{
         y\in P:\langle u_t,\widehat y_o\rangle\ge1-\frac{\eta^2}{2}
      \right\}.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Each sample lands in $A$ with probability at least $\alpha$, so the
   probability that no sample lands in $A$ is at most
   $(1-\alpha)^T\le e^{-\alpha T}$.  If $X_t\in A$, then for every $y\in A$,
   \[
      \langle \widehat X_{t,o},\widehat y_o\rangle
      =
      1-\frac12\|\widehat X_{t,o}-\widehat y_o\|^2
      \ge
      1-\frac{\eta^2}{2},
   \]
   which is the displayed cap containment.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Dual-weighted cap seeding]\label{cor:dual-weighted-cap-seeding}
   Fix point prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$, pattern prices $\lambda_a\ge0$, and a
   storage price $\sigma\ge0$, with $\sum_{x\in P}\alpha_x>0$.  Draw seed
   directions from $P$ with probability proportional to $\alpha_x$, and suppose
   a set $A\subseteq P$ has price mass
   \[
      \alpha(A):=\sum_{x\in A}\alpha_x
      \ge \theta\sum_{x\in P}\alpha_x .
   \]
   Assume that every seed $x\in A$ generates an accepted certified parent
   proposal $C_x$ containing $A$.  If, for each such proposal, $k=|A|$ is
   atom-safe and
   \[
      \alpha(A)>
      \Psi(R_{C_x})
      \left(
         \sigma k^{1+\rho}
         +
         k^\rho\sum_a\lambda_a a_{C_x}
      \right),
   \]
   then $T$ independent dual-weighted samples find a positive-gain atom-safe
   subchild with probability at least $1-e^{-\theta T}$.  Exhaustive
   enumeration of all data directions finds one deterministically.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   With probability at least $1-e^{-\theta T}$, one sampled seed lies in $A$.
   For that accepted parent $C_x$, the top-$k$ prefix by point price has price
   at least $\alpha(A)$ because $A\subseteq P_{C_x}$ and $|A|=k$.  The displayed
   inequality is therefore exactly the positive-gain condition in
   \cref{cor:price-density-subchild}.  Exhaustive enumeration includes every
   seed in $A$, so it cannot miss the same certificate.
\end{proof}

Taking $a_x$ to be the squared distance from $\widehat x_o$ to the current
candidate directions gives the spherical analogue of $k$-means++ seeding.
Taking $a_x$ to be an uncovered near-pair or active-power residual gives the
score-power version.  Taking $a_x$ from the current dual point prices gives the
column-generation version in \cref{cor:dual-weighted-cap-seeding}.  These
statements are deliberately weaker than a Lloyd guarantee: they only say that
any cap carrying a noticeable fraction of the current residual or dual mass is
proposed after logarithmically many samples.  That is the property needed by the
certified-cover route, because the proposed cap is subsequently verified and
used only if its active-power certificate is good.  The remaining worst-case
theorem is therefore not a statement about the convergence of Lloyd's objective;
it is a statement that every tame node exposes enough residual or dual price
mass on certifiable low-stabbing caps, or else the guard or baseline score
process handles the query.

\begin{lemma}[Weak residual-cover compiler]\label{lem:weak-residual-cover}
   Let $P$ be finite with weights $a_x>0$, and let
   $a(U)=\sum_{x\in U}a_x$.  Consider the following abstract residual-cover
   procedure.  At round $t$, with uncovered set $U_t$, draw $T$ independent
   samples from $U_t$ with probabilities $a_x/a(U_t)$, generate the
   corresponding cap or score-filter proposals, and keep only the proposals
   accepted by a deterministic verifier.  Accepted proposals may cover any
   subsets of $U_t$, and covered points are removed before the next round.

   Suppose that for some $\alpha,\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ the following weak
   residual condition holds at every possible round: whenever
   $a(U_t)>\varepsilon a(P)$, there is a set $A_t\subseteq U_t$ with
   \[
      a(A_t)\ge\alpha a(U_t)
   \]
   such that every sample $x\in A_t$ generates an accepted proposal covering
   all of $A_t$.  If
   \[
      H\ge \alpha^{-1}\log\frac1\varepsilon,
      \qquad
      T\ge \alpha^{-1}\log\frac{H}{\delta},
   \]
   then after $H$ rounds the uncovered mass is at most
   $\varepsilon a(P)$ with probability at least $1-\delta$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Fix a round with $a(U_t)>\varepsilon a(P)$.  By the weak residual
   condition, one draw lands in $A_t$ with probability at least $\alpha$.
   Hence the probability that none of the $T$ samples lands in $A_t$ is at most
   \[
      (1-\alpha)^T\le e^{-\alpha T}\le\frac{\delta}{H}.
   \]
   On the complementary event, the verifier accepts a proposal covering all of
   $A_t$, so
   \[
      a(U_{t+1})\le a(U_t)-a(A_t)\le(1-\alpha)a(U_t).
   \]
   A union bound over the $H$ rounds shows that, with probability at least
   $1-\delta$, every nonterminal round decreases residual mass by this factor.
   Therefore either the process has already reached
   $a(U_t)\le\varepsilon a(P)$, or after $H$ successful rounds
   \[
      a(U_H)\le(1-\alpha)^H a(P)
      \le e^{-\alpha H}a(P)
      \le \varepsilon a(P).
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Exhaustive residual-cover compiler]\label{lem:exhaustive-residual-cover}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:weak-residual-cover}, replace the $T$ random
   samples at each round by the exhaustive finite candidate pool generated by
   every point $x\in U_t$.  Under the same weak residual condition, after
   \[
      H\ge \alpha^{-1}\log\frac1\varepsilon
   \]
   rounds the uncovered mass is deterministically at most
   $\varepsilon a(P)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If $a(U_t)\le\varepsilon a(P)$ there is nothing to prove.  Otherwise the
   weak residual condition gives a set $A_t\subseteq U_t$ with
   $a(A_t)\ge\alpha a(U_t)$ such that every sample point in $A_t$ generates an
   accepted proposal covering $A_t$.  Exhaustive enumeration includes all
   points of $A_t$, hence at least one accepted proposal covering $A_t$ is
   available.  Keeping such a proposal gives
   \[
      a(U_{t+1})\le a(U_t)-a(A_t)\le(1-\alpha)a(U_t).
   \]
   Iterating for $H$ rounds yields
   $a(U_H)\le e^{-\alpha H}a(P)\le\varepsilon a(P)$ unless the process stopped
   earlier.
\end{proof}

Combining \cref{lem:residual-cap-seeding} with
\cref{lem:weak-residual-cover} gives the precise analyzable replacement for
Lloyd iterations.  If every residual with nonnegligible mass contains an
$\alpha$-heavy low-diameter cap whose verified filter has acceptable radius,
storage increment, and marginal active-power increment, then residual sampling
constructs the local cover after
\[
   O\!\left(
      \alpha^{-2}\log\frac1\varepsilon
      \log\frac{\alpha^{-1}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\delta}
   \right)
\]
verified proposals.  Thus inverse-polylogarithmic weak cap mass gives only
polylogarithmic preprocessing overhead.  The real geometric statement needed
for \cref{asm:power-cover} is now sharper: prove this weak residual condition
for the active-power verifier, not convergence of the Lloyd objective.
\Cref{lem:exhaustive-residual-cover} gives the build-time-heavy variant: the
same weak condition can be certified over the finite pool of all residual data
directions, with no sampling failure and no dependence on Lloyd dynamics.

The same viewpoint separates build time from the theorem's two scarce
resources, index space and query time.  A proposal may be expensive to find and
expensive to certify; if it is not stored, it contributes nothing to either
resource.
In the offline cover route below, preprocessing time is therefore not a
complexity parameter.  It may be superpolynomial in the local node size, may
solve exact integer or fractional programs, and may try many randomized or
learned proposal generators.  The stated ANN bounds count only the certified
children, residual scans, and decoder operations that remain in the final
index.

\begin{lemma}[Offline proposal amplification]\label{lem:offline-amplification}
   Fix deterministic predicates $\mathcal P(L,S)$ certifying that a stored
   cover is routing-correct, has active query power at most $L$ for every
   query in the promised node, and has storage power at most $S$.  Suppose a
   randomized preprocessing routine outputs such a certified cover with
   probability at least $p$, and otherwise either rejects or outputs a cover
   that fails the certificate and is discarded.  After $B$ independent offline
   attempts, keeping the first certified cover succeeds with probability at
   least
   \[
      1-(1-p)^B .
   \]
   Conditional on success, the query and storage bounds are exactly those of
   the single retained certified cover; rejected proposals and failed attempts
   do not affect the index.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The probability that all $B$ independent attempts fail to produce a certified
   cover is at most $(1-p)^B$.  On the complementary event, the data structure
   stores one cover satisfying $\mathcal P(L,S)$.  Query time and storage are
   functions only of this retained cover and its certificate, not of the
   discarded build-time proposals.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite-pool offline optimizer]\label{lem:finite-pool-optimizer}
   Fix a finite proposal pool of certified Lipschitz score filters
   \[
      \mathcal F=\{(M_i,o_i,R_i,\phi_i,\theta_i,L_i):i\in I\},
   \]
   where
   \[
      M_i\subseteq
      P\cap\Ball(o_i,R_i)\cap\{x:\phi_i(x)\ge\theta_i\}
   \]
   is the admissible membership set of filter $i$.  Also fix a deterministic
   verifier which, given a residual block $U_0$ and selected memberships
   $P_i\subseteq M_i$, accepts only if the selected filters cover
   $P\setminus U_0$, the residual size bound holds, and the active query-power
   and storage-power certificates required by \cref{asm:weak-offline-score-cover}
   hold.

   If some choice of $U_0$ and memberships from the pool is accepted by this
   verifier, then unbounded preprocessing can find an accepted choice by
   exhaustive search, or equivalently by an exact finite integer optimization
   over the membership variables.  The query time and index space are those of
   the accepted stored cover only; the size of $\mathcal F$ and the cost of the
   search are preprocessing costs.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The pool is finite, and each point-filter membership is either selected or
   not selected.  Therefore the number of possible residual blocks and selected
   membership patterns is finite.  Exhaustive preprocessing enumerates them,
   runs the deterministic verifier, and keeps the first accepted pattern.  If an
   accepted pattern exists, the enumeration eventually finds one.  Once the
   index is built, the discarded proposals and rejected membership patterns are
   absent from the data structure, so they do not contribute to query work or
   storage.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Offline Pareto compression]\label{cor:offline-pareto-compression}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:finite-pool-optimizer}, suppose the verifier
   also reports the residual size, the active-power vector on the finite
   query-pattern certificate, and the storage power of every accepted retained
   family.  If at least one retained family is accepted, then unbounded
   preprocessing can choose an accepted family minimizing any prescribed
   function of these reported quantities.  In particular, for fixed residual
   and active-power budgets it can choose a minimum-storage accepted cover, or
   for fixed residual and storage budgets it can choose a cover minimizing the
   worst certified active power.

   The proposal pool, failed randomized attempts, rejected children, and the
   search over retained subfamilies are not stored in the final data structure.
   Only the chosen certified family contributes to index space and query time.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The set of possible retained families and residual blocks is finite.  Run
   the deterministic verifier on every possibility, discard all rejected
   possibilities, and optimize the prescribed objective over the remaining
   finite set.  The final index contains only the selected retained family and
   its decoder.
\end{proof}

By \cref{lem:guard-restricted-cover}, the finite verifier and the LPs below do
not need to impose active-power constraints on high-guard queries.  For a fixed
guard threshold $\gamma$, take $\mathcal Q$ to be a finite set of constraints
from the low-guard domain $\Omega_{\rm low}=\{q:g(q)<\gamma\}$, or take the
activation-pattern set only over this domain.  High-guard queries are handled
by the resident sample and receive no dual price.  This strictly narrows the
remaining separation theorem: a bad dual certificate prices only low-guard
queries, where no large set of already-valid resident answers is available.
By \cref{lem:low-guard-shadow}, the point side can be narrowed as well.  Let
\[
   E_\gamma=\{p\in P:\exists q\in\Omega_{\rm low},\ \|p-q\|\le r\}.
\]
The finite cover program may cover only $E_\gamma$ up to the residual block,
and the dual point prices $\alpha_x$ may be supported only on $E_\gamma$.
Resident points outside this shadow are solved, whenever they matter, by the
high-guard sample.  Thus the remaining separation theorem is a genuinely
low-density statement: it must defeat dual prices on points that can be near a
low-guard query, not on dense regions already certified by the guard.

\begin{lemma}[Finite-pool fractional dual]\label{lem:finite-pool-dual}
   Fix a finite set $\mathcal Q$ of query constraints and a finite proposal pool
   $\mathcal C$ of certified children.  A child $C\in\mathcal C$ consists of a
   point set $P_C\subseteq P$, a certified radius $R_C$, and an activation rule
   on $\mathcal Q$.  Write
   \[
      a_C(q)=\mathbf 1\{C\text{ is active at }q\},
      \qquad
      w_C=|P_C|^\rho\Psi(R_C),
      \qquad
      s_C=|P_C|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_C).
   \]
   For budgets $L_q$, $S$, and $L_0$, consider the fractional cover value
   \[
   \begin{array}{ll}
   \text{\rm maximize} & \displaystyle \sum_{x\in P} v_x \\[0.4em]
   \text{\rm subject to}
      & \displaystyle
        v_x\le \sum_{C:x\in P_C} z_C
        \qquad (x\in P),\\[0.8em]
      & 0\le v_x\le1,\qquad z_C\ge0,\\[0.4em]
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_C a_C(q)w_Cz_C\le L_q
        \qquad (q\in\mathcal Q),\\[0.8em]
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_C s_Cz_C\le S .
   \end{array}
   \tag{$\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal Q}$}
   \]
   If $\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal Q}$ has value $< |P|-L_0$, then there exist
   point prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$, query prices $\lambda_q\ge0$, and a storage
   price $\sigma\ge0$ such that
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P}\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S,
      \tag{D1}
   \]
   while every proposal satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
      \le
      \sigma s_C+
      w_C\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q} \lambda_q a_C(q).
      \tag{D2}
   \]
   Conversely, if such prices do not exist, then the fractional pool contains a
   cover of value at least $|P|-L_0$ under the displayed query and storage
   budgets.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The dual of $\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal Q}$ has variables $\alpha_x\ge0$ for the
   constraints $v_x\le\sum_{C:x\in P_C}z_C$, variables $\mu_x\ge0$ for
   $v_x\le1$, query variables $\lambda_q\ge0$, and storage variable
   $\sigma\ge0$.  It is
   \[
   \begin{array}{ll}
   \text{\rm minimize}
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_{x\in P}\mu_x
        +\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S\\[0.8em]
   \text{\rm subject to}
      & \alpha_x+\mu_x\ge1
        \qquad (x\in P),\\[0.4em]
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
        \le
        \sigma s_C+
        w_C\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_q a_C(q)
        \qquad (C\in\mathcal C).
   \end{array}
   \]
   Since the primal is feasible and bounded, strong duality applies.  In an
   optimal dual solution we may assume $0\le\alpha_x\le1$ and
   $\mu_x=1-\alpha_x$.  Therefore the dual objective is
   \[
      |P|-\sum_x\alpha_x+
      \sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S .
   \]
   Having primal value below $|P|-L_0$ is exactly the existence of a dual
   feasible solution for which this objective is below $|P|-L_0$, which is
   equivalent to \((D1)\).  The remaining dual constraints are precisely
   \((D2)\).  The converse is the same duality implication read backward.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Shadow finite-pool dual]\label{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual}
   Fix a target set $E\subseteq P$, a finite low-guard query constraint set
   $\mathcal Q$, and a finite proposal pool whose children are hereditary under
   taking subsets.  Replace every proposal $C$ by the exposed subchild with
   point set $P_C\cap E$, discard empty subchildren, and compute its query and
   storage weights using the exposed cardinality:
   \[
      w_{C,E}=|P_C\cap E|^\rho\Psi(R_C),
      \qquad
      s_{C,E}=|P_C\cap E|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_C).
   \]
   Consider the fractional cover program that maximizes
   $\sum_{x\in E}v_x$, covers only points of $E$, and uses the active and
   storage weights $w_{C,E},s_{C,E}$.  If its value is below $|E|-L_0$, then
   there are prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$ supported on $E$, query prices
   $\lambda_q\ge0$, and a storage price $\sigma\ge0$ such that
   \[
      \sum_{x\in E}\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S,
   \]
   while every proposal satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P_C\cap E}\alpha_x
      \le
      \sigma s_{C,E}
      +
      w_{C,E}\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_q a_C(q).
   \]
   Conversely, if no such supported price system exists, the exposed pool has a
   fractional cover of $E$ with residual value at most $L_0$ under the displayed
   budgets.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} with ground set $E$ and with the proposal
   pool consisting of the exposed subchildren $P_C\cap E$.  Heredity guarantees
   that each exposed subchild keeps the same radius and activation predicate
   and is still certified.  The displayed inequalities are exactly the dual
   certificate from \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} after extending the point prices
   by zero on $P\setminus E$.
\end{proof}

The dual gives a concrete way to attack the remaining geometric problem.  A
failure of the finite fractional cover produces a weighted residual set
$\alpha$, a query-load distribution $\lambda$, and a storage price $\sigma$ such
that every certified proposal has less $\alpha$-mass than its charged storage
and query footprint.  Thus the desired local theorem can be phrased without any
reference to Lloyd dynamics: for a rich enough proposal pool, rule out all
price systems satisfying \((D1)\)--\((D2)\), or the shadow-supported version in
\cref{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual}, then use the offline optimizer and
deterministic verifier to retain only a low-stabbing cover.  In the
guard-restricted route the relevant target set is the low-guard shadow
$E_\gamma$, so dense regions outside $E_\gamma$ never need to be bought by a
dual witness or stored inside an exposed child.

\begin{corollary}[Dual-price proposal separation]\label{cor:dual-price-separation}
   Fix a finite universe $\mathcal C_\star$ of certified candidate children and
   the same finite query constraint set $\mathcal Q$.  For prices
   $(\alpha,\lambda,\sigma)$ define the reduced gain of a child $C$ by
   \[
      \operatorname{gain}_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}(C)
      =
      \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
      -
      \sigma s_C
      -
      w_C\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_q a_C(q).
   \]
   The full fractional program over $\mathcal C_\star$ has value at least
   $|P|-L_0$ if and only if every price system with
   \[
      \sum_x\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S,
      \qquad
      0\le\alpha_x\le1,\quad \lambda_q,\sigma\ge0,
   \]
   has some child $C\in\mathcal C_\star$ with
   \[
      \operatorname{gain}_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}(C)>0.
   \]
   Consequently, with unbounded preprocessing, a column-generation routine that
   repeatedly solves the current finite-pool LP and adds any positive-gain child
   from $\mathcal C_\star$ terminates with value at least $|P|-L_0$ whenever the
   full universe satisfies this separation property.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} to the full pool
   $\mathcal C_\star$.  Failure of value at least $|P|-L_0$ is exactly the
   existence of prices satisfying the displayed surplus inequality and
   \(\operatorname{gain}_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}(C)\le0\) for every
   $C\in\mathcal C_\star$.  Negating this statement gives the equivalence.

   For the algorithmic statement, $\mathcal C_\star$ is finite.  Each positive
   reduced-gain child violates the current dual certificate, so adding it either
   increases the current pool or the procedure has already reached the target
   value.  The routine therefore terminates after at most $|\mathcal C_\star|$
   additions, and the equivalence rules out termination below value
   $|P|-L_0$ under the separation property.
\end{proof}

This is the proof-aligned analogue of $k$-means++ seeding.  Instead of sampling
from squared-distance residuals, preprocessing may solve the current fractional
cover, read the dual prices, and search the very large proposal universe for a
positive-gain cap, medoid ball, pair-direction filter, or learned score.  The
search may be arbitrarily expensive; the retained index still pays only for the
children that survive the final deterministic certificate.

\begin{lemma}[Finite activation-pattern verifier]\label{lem:finite-activation-patterns}
   Fix a finite retained family of certified children $\mathcal C$ and a query
   domain $\Omega$.  Each child $C$ has an activation predicate
   $a_C(q)\in\{0,1\}$.  Let
   \[
      \mathcal A(\mathcal C)
      =
      \bigl\{
         (a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C}:q\in\Omega
      \bigr\}
      \subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal C}
   \]
   be the set of realizable activation patterns.  For any nonnegative child
   weights $w_C$,
   \[
      \sup_{q\in\Omega}\sum_{C\in\mathcal C}a_C(q)w_C
      =
      \max_{a\in\mathcal A(\mathcal C)}
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal C}a_Cw_C .
   \]
   Consequently an all-query active-power certificate for a finite retained
   family is exactly a finite list of linear constraints, one for each
   realizable activation pattern.  Since
   $|\mathcal A(\mathcal C)|\le2^{|\mathcal C|}$, unbounded preprocessing may
   verify the certificate by enumerating realizable patterns.  It is also safe
   to enumerate any finite superset of the realizable patterns; this can only
   make the certificate stricter.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The displayed equality is just grouping queries by their activation vector:
   the summand depends on $q$ only through $(a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C}$.  The
   remaining claims follow because the set of binary activation vectors of a
   finite family is finite, and a finite superset of the realizable vectors
   contains the maximum over the realizable ones.
\end{proof}

For the cap, shell-normalized cap, and ball predicates above, the activation
sets are finite Boolean combinations of explicit inequalities.  Thus the
pattern enumeration is a preprocessing certification problem.  The paper does
not need this enumeration to be efficient: after the certificate is accepted,
the query algorithm only evaluates its ordinary decoder and pays for the listed
children.

\begin{lemma}[Atom-safe subchild separation]\label{lem:atom-safe-subchild}
   Fix finite query-pattern budgets $L_q>0$ and a storage budget $S>0$.  Let
   $C$ be any certified child with point set $P_C$, radius $R_C$, and activation
   vector $a_C(q)$ on the finite query set.  Write
   \[
      \Lambda_C=\sum_q\lambda_q a_C(q),
      \qquad
      \psi_C=\Psi(R_C).
   \]
   A subchild $D\subseteq P_C$ inherits the same radius and activation rule and
   has weights
   \[
      w_D=|D|^\rho\psi_C,
      \qquad
      s_D=|D|^{1+\rho}\psi_C .
   \]
   It is atom-safe if
   \[
      a_C(q)w_D\le L_q\quad(q\in\mathcal Q),
      \qquad
      s_D\le S .
   \]
   For fixed dual prices $(\alpha,\lambda,\sigma)$, the maximum reduced gain
   over all atom-safe subchildren of $C$ is obtained by sorting the points of
   $P_C$ in decreasing order of $\alpha_x$ and trying the prefixes of all
   atom-safe cardinalities.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Any $D\subseteq P_C$ remains certified because the ball and score
   constraints are hereditary under taking subsets.  For a subchild of
   cardinality $k$, the non-point part of the reduced gain is fixed:
   \[
      -\sigma k^{1+\rho}\psi_C
      -
      k^\rho\psi_C\Lambda_C .
   \]
   Thus, among all subchildren of size $k$, the one maximizing reduced gain is
   exactly the set of the $k$ largest point prices $\alpha_x$ inside $P_C$.
   The atom-safety constraints also depend only on $k$, $R_C$, and the activation
   vector of $C$.  Trying all feasible cardinalities therefore finds the best
   atom-safe subchild.
\end{proof}

Thus the bounded-atom hypothesis in
\cref{lem:fractional-cover-materialization} is not a new geometric object.  It
is a preprocessing normal form: replace a rich proposal universe by the finite
universe of all atom-safe certified subchildren.  Under dual prices, separation
against this expanded universe reduces to the same geometric proposal search,
followed by the prefix test of \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}.

\begin{corollary}[Price-density subchild certificate]
   \label{cor:price-density-subchild}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}, write the point prices in
   $P_C$ in nonincreasing order
   \[
      \alpha_1\ge\alpha_2\ge\cdots\ge\alpha_M,
      \qquad M=|P_C|,
   \]
   and put $A_k=\sum_{j\le k}\alpha_j$.  If some atom-safe cardinality $k$
   satisfies
   \[
      A_k>
      \psi_C\bigl(\sigma k^{1+\rho}+\Lambda_C k^\rho\bigr),
      \tag{PD}
   \]
   then the expanded atom-safe universe contains a positive-gain child.
   Conversely, if no atom-safe subchild of $C$ has positive gain, then every
   atom-safe cardinality $k$ satisfies
   \[
      \frac{A_k}{k}
      \le
      \psi_C\bigl(\sigma k^\rho+\Lambda_C k^{\rho-1}\bigr).
      \tag{PD$^\ast$}
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}, the best atom-safe subchild of size $k$ is
   the set of the $k$ largest point prices.  Its reduced gain is
   \[
      A_k-\sigma\psi_C k^{1+\rho}-\Lambda_C\psi_C k^\rho .
   \]
   This is positive exactly when \((PD)\) holds.  Dividing the nonpositivity of
   this expression by $k$ gives \((PD^\ast)\).
\end{proof}

In the low-guard shadow program, \cref{cor:price-density-subchild} is applied to
the exposed parent $P_C\cap E_\gamma$ and to prices supported on
$E_\gamma$.  Unexposed points are discarded before the prefix test.  Thus a
candidate parent proposal is useful exactly when some prefix of its exposed
high-price points beats the storage and active-pattern charge after the
cardinality, query weight, and storage weight have all been computed on that
exposed prefix.  This prevents dense but guard-solved points from diluting the
price density of a proposal.

\begin{lemma}[Averaged proposal separation]\label{lem:averaged-proposal-separation}
   Fix any dual price system and any finite certified child universe
   $\mathcal C_\star$.  Let $\Pi$ be a probability distribution on
   $\mathcal C_\star$, and write
   \[
      \Lambda_C=\sum_q\lambda_qa_C(q)
   \]
   for the query-price load of $C$.  If
   \[
      \E_{C\sim\Pi}\left[
         \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
         -\sigma s_C
         -w_C\Lambda_C
      \right]>0,
   \]
   then some child in $\mathcal C_\star$ has positive reduced gain.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If every child had nonpositive reduced gain, every average of reduced gains
   would be nonpositive.  The displayed strict positivity contradicts this.
\end{proof}

Thus a randomized build-time proposal mechanism can be used as a dual
separator without analyzing its convergence as a stored algorithm.  Given dual
prices, it is enough to produce a distribution over verified parent proposals
whose best atom-safe price prefix has positive expected gain.  Unbounded
preprocessing then samples or enumerates until it finds the positive-gain
certificate, adds that column, and discards all failed attempts.  The remaining
geometric theorem for cap or learned-score proposals can therefore be stated in
the price-density form \((PD)\): every surplus dual price system must expose a
certified radius-contracting proposal with enough high-price prefix mass to
beat its storage price and active-pattern price.

\begin{lemma}[Fractional cover materialization]\label{lem:fractional-cover-materialization}
   In the finite program of \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual}, assume $L_q>0$ for all
   $q\in\mathcal Q$, $S>0$, and the following atom bounds:
   \[
      a_C(q)w_C\le L_q \qquad(C\in\mathcal C,\ q\in\mathcal Q),
      \qquad
      s_C\le S \qquad(C\in\mathcal C).
   \]
   Suppose there is a feasible fractional solution $(z_C,v_x)$ of value at
   least $|P|-L_0$.  Let
   \[
      B\ge
      \max\left\{
         1,\log(4|P|),\log(8(|\mathcal Q|+1))
      \right\}.
   \]
   Then there exists an integral subfamily $\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal C$ and a
   residual set
   \[
      U_0=P\setminus\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal A}P_C
   \]
   such that
   \[
      |U_0|\le 4(L_0+1),
   \]
   and, simultaneously for every $q\in\mathcal Q$,
   \[
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal A}a_C(q)w_C\le 3B L_q,
      \qquad
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal A}s_C\le 3B S .
   \]
   Thus a finite fractional score cover can be stored as an ordinary certified
   cover with only logarithmic budget inflation and a comparable residual scan.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Put
   \[
      y_x=\sum_{C:x\in P_C}z_C .
   \]
   Since $v_x\le1$ and $v_x\le y_x$, the value assumption implies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P}\bigl(1-\min(1,y_x)\bigr)
      \le
      \sum_{x\in P}(1-v_x)
      \le L_0 .
   \]
   Independently keep each child $C$ with probability
   \[
      p_C=\min(1,Bz_C).
   \]
   If no child containing $x$ has $p_C=1$, then
   \[
      \Pr[x\notin\cup_{C\in\mathcal A}P_C]
      \le
      \exp(-B y_x).
   \]
   If some containing child has $p_C=1$, the probability is zero.  Hence in all
   cases
   \[
      \Pr[x\in U_0]
      \le
      1-\min(1,y_x)+e^{-B}.
   \]
   Indeed, for $y_x\ge1$ the exponential bound is at most $e^{-B}$, while for
   $y_x\le1$ convexity gives
   $e^{-B y_x}\le 1-y_x+y_xe^{-B}\le1-y_x+e^{-B}$.
   Summing over $x$ and using $B\ge\log(4|P|)$ gives
   \[
      \E |U_0|\le L_0+\frac14 .
   \]
   By Markov's inequality,
   \[
      \Pr[|U_0|>4(L_0+1)]<\frac14 .
   \]

   For a fixed query $q$, define
   \[
      X_q=\sum_{C\in\mathcal A}a_C(q)w_C .
   \]
   The atom bound makes $X_q/L_q$ a sum of independent random variables in
   $[0,1]$, and
   \[
      \E X_q\le
      \sum_C a_C(q)w_C Bz_C
      \le B L_q .
   \]
   The standard Chernoff bound for sums of independent $[0,1]$ variables gives
   \[
      \Pr[X_q>3B L_q]\le e^{-B}.
   \]
   The same argument applied to
   $X_{\rm store}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal A}s_C$ gives
   \[
      \Pr[X_{\rm store}>3B S]\le e^{-B}.
   \]
   Since $B\ge\log(8(|\mathcal Q|+1))$, a union bound over all query constraints
   and the storage constraint has failure probability at most $1/4$.  With
   positive probability neither the residual event nor any budget event fails,
   so an integral subfamily with the displayed guarantees exists.  Unbounded
   preprocessing may find it by trying the random choices until the deterministic
   certificate passes, or by exhaustive search over the retained subfamilies.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Pattern-complete fractional cover]\label{lem:pattern-complete-cover}
   Fix a finite universe $\mathcal C_\star$ of certified children and a query
   domain $\Omega$.  Let
   \[
      \mathcal A_\star
      =
      \{(a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C_\star}:q\in\Omega\}
      \subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal C_\star}
   \]
   be the realizable activation patterns of the full proposal universe, or let
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}\supseteq\mathcal A_\star$ be any finite superset of
   patterns.  Consider the fractional cover program of
   \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} with one query constraint for each
   $a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}$,
   \[
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal C_\star} a_C w_C z_C\le L,
   \]
   and with storage budget $S$ and residual budget $L_0$.  If this program has
   value at least $|P|-L_0$ and the atom bounds
   \[
      a_Cw_C\le L\qquad(a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A},\,C\in\mathcal C_\star),
      \qquad
      s_C\le S
   \]
   hold, then an integral stored subfamily $\mathcal R\subseteq\mathcal C_\star$
   and residual block $U_0$ exist with
   \[
      |U_0|\le4(L_0+1),
      \qquad
      \sup_{q\in\Omega}\sum_{C\in\mathcal R}a_C(q)w_C\le3BL,
      \qquad
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal R}s_C\le3BS,
   \]
   where
   \[
      B\ge
      \max\{1,\log(4|P|),\log(8(|\widetilde{\mathcal A}|+1))\}.
   \]
   Conversely, if the fractional value is below $|P|-L_0$, then there are point
   prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$, pattern prices $\lambda_a\ge0$, and a storage
   price $\sigma\ge0$ such that
   \[
      \sum_x\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+L\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a+\sigma S
   \]
   and every proposal has nonpositive pattern-priced gain,
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
      \le
      \sigma s_C
      +
      w_C\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a a_C
      \qquad(C\in\mathcal C_\star).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Treat each activation pattern $a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ as one query
   constraint in \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} and
   \cref{lem:fractional-cover-materialization}.  The rounding lemma gives an
   integral subfamily with residual, storage, and pattern-load bounds
   simultaneously for every $a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}$.  Since each actual
   query $q\in\Omega$ realizes a pattern in
   $\mathcal A_\star\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal A}$, the same bound holds for
   $\sup_q\sum_Ca_C(q)w_C$.  The displayed dual certificate is exactly
   \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} with the query index set replaced by
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ and all query budgets equal to $L$.
\end{proof}

The factor $B$ is an index-size and query-time inflation, not a preprocessing
cost.  Thus this compiler feeds the polylogarithmic theorem only when the
retained activation class has polylogarithmic pattern entropy, for example when
after localization the relevant cap or score predicates have an arrangement
bound with $\log|\widetilde{\mathcal A}|=\log^{O_c(1)}m$.  If the exact pattern
set is much larger, the same LP and dual are still useful as an offline
certificate and separation diagnostic, but direct randomized materialization
would not by itself preserve polylogarithmic overhead; one would then need an
exact integral certificate or a sharper entropy-sensitive rounding theorem.
By contrast, an exact integral search as in
\cref{cor:offline-pareto-compression} pays no rounding factor at all.  Its
pattern enumeration may be enormous, but that cost is purely preprocessing.
Thus large pattern entropy is an obstacle only to proving an integral cover from
a fractional LP with the rounding lemma above; it is not an index-size obstacle
once an integral low-stabbing cover has been certified.

\begin{lemma}[Linear score pattern entropy]\label{lem:linear-pattern-entropy}
   Let $\Omega\subseteq\R^k$, and let the activation predicates be
   \[
      a_i(q)=\mathbf 1\{\langle u_i,q\rangle\ge b_i\},
      \qquad i=1,\ldots,N .
   \]
   Then the number of activation patterns
   \[
      \{(a_1(q),\ldots,a_N(q)):q\in\Omega\}
   \]
   is at most $N^{O(k)}$.  In particular,
   \[
      \log|\mathcal A|\le O(k\log(N+2)).
   \]
   The same bound holds if several certified children share the same
   activation predicate; duplicated predicates do not increase the number of
   realizable binary vectors except by duplicating coordinates.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The binary vector is constant on each relatively open face of the
   arrangement of the $N$ hyperplanes
   $\langle u_i,q\rangle=b_i$, with hyperplanes containing the face counted as
   active because of the weak inequality.  Thus it is enough to bound the number
   of faces of the arrangement.

   We use the standard induction.  When the $N$th hyperplane is inserted, the
   only new faces are obtained by cutting old faces that meet it.  Those cuts
   are controlled by the arrangement induced by the first $N-1$ hyperplanes on
   the new hyperplane, which has dimension at most $k-1$.  If $F(N,k)$ is the
   maximum number of relatively open faces of an arrangement of $N$ hyperplanes
   in $\R^k$, then
   \[
      F(N,k)\le F(N-1,k)+O(F(N-1,k-1)),
      \qquad
      F(N,0)=1,\quad F(0,k)=1 .
   \]
   Induction on $N+k$ gives $F(N,k)\le (CN)^k$ for a universal constant $C$.
   Restricting to $\Omega$ can only decrease the number of realized patterns.
   If predicates are duplicated, every duplicate coordinate is forced to equal
   its original predicate, so no new choices are created.
\end{proof}

Thus cap, shell-normalized cap, and affine score filters have polylogarithmic
pattern entropy after the standard projected localization, provided the number
of distinct activation predicates is $m^{\log^{O_c(1)}m}$ or smaller and the
projected dimension is $\log^{O_c(1)}m$.  The atom-safe subchildren used for
materialization inherit their parent's activation predicate, so they do not
increase this entropy.  Under these conditions \cref{lem:pattern-complete-cover}
contributes only another polylogarithmic factor.

Thus for modern regimes where preprocessing compute is relatively cheap, the
right theoretical target is even cleaner: generate a very large candidate pool
by residual sampling, all data directions, medoids, pair directions, or learned
scores; solve the active-power assignment problem as accurately as desired; and
store only a cover that passes the deterministic certificate.  Expensive build
time can amplify small discovery probabilities or replace greedy assignment by
exact integer programming over the candidate pool.  What remains for the
polylogarithmic ANN theorem is an existence-and-certification statement for a
small stored cover, not an efficient Lloyd-style training algorithm.

\begin{lemma}[Shadow price-density compiler]\label{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler}
   Work in one bounded-diameter node with resident set $P$, $|P|=m$, radius
   $R$, guard threshold $\gamma$, low-guard domain $\Omega_{\rm low}$, and
   exposed shadow $E=E_\gamma$.  Let $\mathcal C_0$ be a finite universe of
   certified Lipschitz-score parent proposals.  Every parent $C\in\mathcal C_0$
   has point set $P_C$, radius $R_C\le R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$, activation predicate
   $a_C(q)$ on $\Omega_{\rm low}$, and hereditary certification under taking
   subsets.  Let $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ be a finite superset of the
   low-guard activation patterns of these parents.  When parent proposals are
   expanded into atom-safe subchildren below, each parent coordinate is
   duplicated for all its subchildren, and we use the same symbol
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ for the lifted pattern set.  Set
   \[
      B\ge
      \max\{1,\log(4(|E|+1)),\log(8(|\widetilde{\mathcal A}|+1))\}.
   \]
   Fix budgets $L>0$, $S>0$, and $L_0\ge0$.

   Suppose the following shadow separation property holds.  For every choice
   of point prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$ supported on $E$, pattern prices
   $\lambda_a\ge0$, and storage price $\sigma\ge0$ with surplus
   \[
      \sum_{x\in E}\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+L\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a+\sigma S,
      \tag{S1}
   \]
   there is a parent proposal $C\in\mathcal C_0$ and an atom-safe cardinality
   $k$ for its exposed set $P_C\cap E$ such that, after sorting the exposed
   points of $P_C\cap E$ by decreasing $\alpha_x$ and writing
   $A_k$ for the prefix sum,
   \[
      A_k>
      \Psi(R_C)
      \left(
         \sigma k^{1+\rho}
         +
         k^\rho
         \sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a a_C
      \right).
      \tag{S2}
   \]
   Here atom-safe means that the exposed prefix of size $k$ has storage at most
   $S$ and active weight at most $L$ on every pattern in
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$.

   Then unbounded preprocessing can build an integral retained family
   $\mathcal R$ of exposed subchildren and a residual block $U_0\subseteq E$
   such that
   \[
      |U_0|\le4(L_0+1),
      \qquad
      \sup_{q\in\Omega_{\rm low}}
      \sum_{D\in\mathcal R}a_D(q)|P_D|^\rho\Psi(R_D)
      \le3BL,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{D\in\mathcal R}|P_D|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_D)\le3BS .
   \]
   The retained children cover $E\setminus U_0$, are certified, and all have
   radius at most $R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Expand the proposal universe as follows.  For every parent
   $C\in\mathcal C_0$, intersect it with $E$, and include every atom-safe
   subset of $P_C\cap E$.  Since $P$ is finite, this expanded universe is
   finite; for any fixed price vector the best atom-safe subchild of a parent
   is the prefix identified by \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}.  Each exposed
   subchild inherits the parent's activation predicate and radius certificate.

   Lift $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ to the expanded universe by duplicating each
   parent coordinate for all exposed subchildren of that parent.  Run the
   pattern-complete fractional cover program on this expanded exposed universe,
   with the resident ground set in \cref{lem:pattern-complete-cover} replaced by
   $E$, pattern set $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$, active budget $L$, storage budget
   $S$, and residual budget $L_0$.  If its value were below $|E|-L_0$,
   \cref{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual}, applied with query constraints indexed by
   the lifted patterns, would give prices satisfying \((S1)\) and having
   nonpositive gain on every exposed atom-safe subchild.  But by the assumed
   separation property, some parent has an atom-safe exposed prefix satisfying
   \((S2)\), which is exactly positive reduced gain for that subchild.  This
   contradiction proves that the fractional value is at least $|E|-L_0$.

   Apply \cref{lem:pattern-complete-cover} to materialize the fractional exposed
   cover.  The atom-safety hypotheses are part of the expanded universe, and
   duplicated activation predicates do not change the pattern set.  The
   materialized family covers all but $4(L_0+1)$ exposed points, has active
   pattern load at most $3BL$, and has storage at most $3BS$.  Since every
   actual low-guard query realizes a pattern in
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$, the same active bound holds uniformly over
   $\Omega_{\rm low}$.  Unbounded preprocessing may find the retained family by
   exhaustive search or by column generation using the positive-gain prefix
   oracle, and stores only the final certified family and residual block.
\end{proof}

\begin{assumption}[Weak offline score-cover condition]\label{asm:weak-offline-score-cover}
   Fix $c>1$, $\rho=\rho_c$, and $\eta>0$.  In every bounded-diameter localized
   node with $m$ resident points in a certified ball of radius $R$, either the
   node is terminal, or expensive preprocessing can build the following
   certified object with constant probability, amplified offline as in
   \cref{lem:offline-amplification}.

   Let
   \[
      \gamma_m=m^{-\rho}\log^{-O_c(1)}m,
      \qquad
      \Omega_{\rm low}=\{q:g(q)<\gamma_m\},
   \]
   and let $E_{\gamma_m}$ be the low-guard shadow from
   \cref{lem:low-guard-shadow}.  There is a residual block
   $U_0\subseteq E_{\gamma_m}$ and a query-decodable family of Lipschitz score
   filters
   \[
      (P_i,o_i,R_i,\phi_i,\theta_i,L_i)_{i\in I}
   \]
   covering $E_{\gamma_m}\setminus U_0$, with
   \[
      |U_0|\le F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m,
      \qquad
      R_i\le R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}.
   \]
   The decoder lists every filter satisfying
   $\phi_i(q)\ge\theta_i-L_i r$ in time
   $dF_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m$ plus output cost.  Unless the resident guard
   density at $q$ is at least $m^{-\rho}\log^{-O_c(1)}m$, the listed filters
   satisfy
   \[
      \sum_{i:\phi_i(q)\ge\theta_i-L_i r}
      |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)
   \]
   for a constant $\kappa=\kappa(c)>0$, and the stored filters satisfy
   \[
      \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)
      \le
      mF_4(m,R).
   \]
   The cover is low-guard routing-correct: if $q\in\Omega_{\rm low}$,
   $\|p-q\|\le r$, and $p\in E_{\gamma_m}$, then $p\in U_0$ or $p$ lies in a
   listed filter.

   Finally, the preprocessing routine may obtain this object by any amount of
   offline computation.  In particular, it is enough that either a residual
   sampler as in \cref{lem:weak-residual-cover}, or exhaustive residual
